#### Week 11: Logic — True/False Statements, Negation and Contrapositive, Antecedent, Consequent and Conditional Statements

**Teaching Notes**

**Simple statements (propositions)** are declarative sentences with a definite truth value — True (T) or False (F), but not both. Represented by lowercase letters p, q, r, s.

Examples: p: "Lagos is a city in Nigeria" (True); q: "The human head is made of wood" (False).

**Not statements**: questions ("What is your name?"), commands ("Pass the salt"), opinions ("This food is delicious"), open sentences ("x + 3 = 8" — depends on x), and exclamations ("Wow!") — none of these have a fixed truth value.

**Negation (¬ or ~)**: the negation of p, written ¬p ("not p"), reverses its truth value.

| p | ¬p |
|---|---|
| T | F |
| F | T |

Rules for forming a correct negation: use "It is not the case that…", insert "not" grammatically, or reverse mathematical relations (> becomes ≤, = becomes ≠, etc.). Careful: the negation of "All cats are black" is "Not all cats are black" (or "Some cats are not black") — NOT "No cats are black."

**Conditional (implication) statements**: "If p, then q," written p → q.
- **p is the antecedent** (the condition).
- **q is the consequent** (the result).
- p → q is False only when p is True and q is False; in every other case (including when p is False) it is considered True.

**Contrapositive** of "if p then q" is "if not q then not p" (¬q → ¬p); a conditional statement and its contrapositive always have the same truth value.

**Other connectives** (relevant background): conjunction (p ∧ q, "and" — true only if both true), disjunction (p ∨ q, "or" — false only if both false), biconditional (p ↔ q, "if and only if" — true when both have the same truth value).

**Worked Example 1.** For each sentence below, decide whether it is a simple (mathematical/logical) statement, and if so, give its truth value.
a) "The year 2024 is a leap year." b) "Solve the equation." c) "Red is a beautiful colour." d) "5 − 1 = 3" e) "x > 10."

**Step 1 — Test sentence (a) against the definition** (a declarative sentence with a fixed, checkable truth value): it declares a fact that can be checked (2024 ÷ 4 = 506 exactly, so 2024 is divisible by 4 and is indeed a leap year) → it IS a statement, and it is **True**.
**Step 2 — Test sentence (b):** it is a command/instruction, not a declaration of fact — commands never have a truth value → it is **NOT a statement**.
**Step 3 — Test sentence (c):** it expresses a personal opinion/taste — different people could disagree with no way to "check" who is right → it is **NOT a statement**.
**Step 4 — Test sentence (d):** it declares a specific arithmetic fact that can be checked: 5 − 1 = 4, not 3 → it IS a statement, and since the claimed equation is wrong, it is **False**.
**Step 5 — Test sentence (e):** "x > 10" contains an unknown x whose value is not fixed — its truth depends entirely on what x turns out to be, so it has no single fixed truth value (this is called an "open sentence") → it is **NOT a statement**.
**Answer: (a) Statement, True. (b) Not a statement (command). (c) Not a statement (opinion). (d) Statement, False. (e) Not a statement (open sentence).**

**Worked Example 2.** Let p: "Lagos is in Ghana" and q: "2 × 3 = 6." Evaluate the conditional statement "If Lagos is in Ghana, then 2×3=6" (p → q).

**Step 1 — Determine the truth value of p on its own:** Lagos is a city in Nigeria, not Ghana, so p is **False**.
**Step 2 — Determine the truth value of q on its own:** 2 × 3 = 6 is a correct arithmetic fact, so q is **True**.
**Step 3 — Recall the rule for a conditional p → q:** it is False ONLY in the case p = True and q = False; in every other combination (including p False) it is True.
**Step 4 — Match our case (p = False, q = True) against the rule:** this is not the "True→False" case, so p → q is True.
**Step 5 — Sanity-check using the truth table row F → T:** by definition, F → T = True.
**Answer: p → q is True** — even though the antecedent (p) is itself false, the whole conditional statement is still true, because a false antecedent makes the implication automatically true (sometimes called "vacuous truth").

**Worked Example 3.** Write the negation of p: "Monday is the first day of the week," and state its truth value.

**Step 1 — Identify what the statement asserts:** that Monday holds the position "first day of the week."
**Step 2 — Form the negation by inserting "not" grammatically (or prefixing "It is not the case that…"):** "Monday is not the first day of the week."
**Step 3 — Determine the truth value of the original p.** Under the international/ISO standard (and Nigerian school convention), the week is considered to start on Monday, so p is **True**.
**Step 4 — Apply the negation rule (¬p reverses p's truth value):** since p is True, ¬p must be **False**.
**Answer: ¬p = "Monday is not the first day of the week," which is False** (since Monday genuinely is the first day of the week).

**Worked Example 4.** Construct a full truth table for the conjunction p ∧ q ("p and q"), and state the one condition under which it is true.

**Step 1 — List every possible combination of truth values for p and q** (there are 2×2 = 4 combinations, since each variable can independently be T or F): (T,T), (T,F), (F,T), (F,F).
**Step 2 — Recall the rule for conjunction (∧, "and"):** p ∧ q is True only when BOTH p and q are True; it is False in every other case.
**Step 3 — Evaluate each row:**

| p | q | p ∧ q |
|---|---|---|
| T | T | T |
| T | F | F |
| F | T | F |
| F | F | F |

**Step 4 — Read off the one row where the result is True:** row 1, where p = T and q = T.
**Answer: p ∧ q is true only when both p and q are true (row T,T); it is false in all three other cases.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Memorise "→ is only False when True leads to False"** — everything else about the conditional (T→T, F→T, F→F are all True) follows from this single exception; you don't need to memorise four separate rules, just the one case that breaks it.
- **For negations of quantified statements ("all," "some," "no"), use the swap table**: negation of "All X are Y" is "Some X are not Y" (NOT "No X are Y"); negation of "Some X are Y" is "No X are Y" / "All X are not Y"; negation of "No X are Y" is "Some X are Y." Memorising this table prevents the classic "all ↔ no" confusion.
- **For negating inequalities, flip the symbol using the complete pairing**: > ↔ ≤, < ↔ ≥, = ↔ ≠. There is no in-between state to worry about — these three pairs cover every case.
- **A statement and its contrapositive are logically twins** — if a question asks you to evaluate a complicated conditional, check whether its contrapositive (¬q → ¬p) is easier to evaluate first; since they always share the same truth value, you can answer via whichever is simpler.
- **Build truth tables systematically in the standard T/F listing order** (TT, TF, FT, FF for two variables) every single time — using a consistent order prevents missed or duplicated rows and makes your table instantly checkable by a teacher or examiner.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Which of these are simple statements: "Good morning!", "5 is a prime number.", "He is very smart."? (answer: only "5 is a prime number" is a statement, and it is True)
Q2. Write the negation of p: "The sun rises in the West." (answer: "The sun does not rise in the West.")
Q3. What is the truth value of the negation in Q2? (answer: True, since p is False)
Q4. Let q be "4 + 4 = 8." State the truth value of q and ¬q. (answer: q = True, ¬q = False)
Q5. Write the negation of r: "x < 5." (answer: "x ≥ 5")
Q6. Write the negation of p: "All birds can fly." (answer: "Not all birds can fly" / "Some birds cannot fly")
Q7. Write the negation of q: "A rhombus is not a square." (answer: "A rhombus is a square")
Q8. Write the negation of r: "12 is a multiple of 5." (answer: "12 is not a multiple of 5")
Q9. Write the negation of s: "A student is present." (answer: "A student is not present")
Q10. State the truth values of the negations in Q6–Q9 given the originals: p (probably intended False, so ¬p True), q (False, so ¬q True), r (True, since 12 is not a multiple of 5, so ¬r False)... (answer: a) True b) False c) True d) depends on context)
Q11. Let p be "x = 3" and q be "x² = 9." If x = 3, what are the truth values of p and q? If x = −3? (answer: x=3: p=True, q=True; x=−3: p=False, q=True)
Q12. Explain why "This statement is false" cannot be a simple statement. (answer: it creates a paradox — if true it says it's false, if false it says it's true, so it has no consistent truth value)
Q13. Identify the antecedent in: "If x = 2, then x² = 4." (answer: "x = 2")
Q14. Let p = "It is sunny" and q = "It is hot." Write "It is not sunny and it is hot" in symbols. (answer: ¬p ∧ q)
Q15. Let p = T and q = T. Find the truth value of p → (¬q). (answer: False)
Q16. Let p = F and q = F. Find the truth value of p → q. (answer: True)
Q17. Let p = "An elephant can fly" (F), q = "A square has 4 sides" (T). Find p ∧ q, p ∨ q, p → q, ¬q → p. (answer: p∧q = F; p∨q = T; p→q = T; ¬q→p = T (since ¬q is F))
Q18. A statement that is always True is called what? Give an example. (answer: a Tautology, e.g. p ∨ ¬p)
Q19. A statement that is always False is called what? Give an example. (answer: a Contradiction, e.g. p ∧ ¬p)
Q20. Construct a truth table for p ∧ ¬q and state when it is true. (answer: true only when p is True and q is False)
Q21. Analyse: "The square root of 16 is 4." Is it a statement? Truth value? (answer: statement; True)
Q22. Analyse: "That movie was boring." Is it a statement? (answer: not a statement — opinion)

### Third Term

#### Week 1: Mensuration of 3-D Shapes I — Total Surface Area (Cube, Cuboid, Cylinder, Cone, Prism, Pyramid)

**Teaching Notes**

**Surface area (SA)** of a solid is the total area of all its faces — the area of its "net" if unfolded flat. It is measured in square units (cm², m²).

**Cube** (side l): TSA = 6l² (6 identical square faces).

**Cuboid** (length l, width w, height h): TSA = 2(lw + lh + wh).

**Cylinder** (radius r, height h): the curved surface, when unrolled, is a rectangle of width 2πr (the circumference) and height h.
- Curved surface area (CSA) = 2πrh
- TSA (closed) = 2πr² + 2πrh = 2πr(r + h)
- TSA (open at one end) = πr² + 2πrh = πr(r + 2h)

**Cone** (base radius R, slant height l, vertical height h): a cone is formed by folding a sector of a circle — the sector's radius becomes the cone's slant height l, and the sector's arc length becomes the cone's base circumference 2πR.
- CSA = πRl
- TSA = πR² + πRl = πR(R + l)
- Slant height, height and radius form a right triangle: **l² = h² + R²**

**Prism** (uniform cross-section, e.g. triangular prism of length L): TSA = 2(base area) + (perimeter of base × L).

**Pyramid** (polygon base, triangular faces meeting at an apex). For a square-based pyramid of base side l and slant height s (the height of a triangular face, not the vertical height): TSA = l² + 2ls (base + 4 triangles, each ½ls).

**Worked example 1:** A closed cylindrical tin has radius 7 cm and height 10 cm (π = 22/7). Find its TSA.
- **Step 1 — Identify the formula:** a closed cylinder has two circular ends plus the curved side, so TSA = 2πr(r + h).
- **Step 2 — Write down the known values:** r = 7 cm, h = 10 cm, π = 22/7.
- **Step 3 — Substitute into the formula:** TSA = 2 × (22/7) × 7 × (7 + 10).
- **Step 4 — Simplify inside the bracket first:** 7 + 10 = 17.
- **Step 5 — Cancel the 7s (one 7 in 22/7 cancels the radius 7):** 2 × 22 × 17.
- **Step 6 — Multiply:** 2 × 22 = 44; 44 × 17 = 748.
- **Answer: TSA = 748 cm².**

**Worked example 2:** A cone has base radius 5 cm and vertical height 12 cm. Find its TSA (leave in terms of π).
- **Step 1 — Recognise what's missing:** the TSA formula πR(R + l) needs the slant height l, which isn't given directly — only R and the vertical height h are given.
- **Step 2 — Find l using the right-triangle relationship inside a cone:** l² = h² + R².
- **Step 3 — Substitute:** l² = 12² + 5² = 144 + 25 = 169.
- **Step 4 — Take the square root:** l = √169 = 13 cm.
- **Step 5 — Substitute R and l into the TSA formula:** TSA = πR(R + l) = π(5)(5 + 13).
- **Step 6 — Simplify the bracket:** 5 + 13 = 18, so TSA = π × 5 × 18 = 90π cm².
- **Step 7 — Convert to a decimal for a sense of scale (using π ≈ 22/7):** 90 × 22/7 ≈ 282.7 cm².
- **Answer: TSA = 90π cm² ≈ 282.7 cm².**

**Worked example 3:** A closed cylinder has diameter 10 cm and height 21 cm (π = 22/7). Find its TSA.
- **Step 1 — Convert diameter to radius (a very common trap):** r = d/2 = 10/2 = 5 cm.
- **Step 2 — Write the closed-cylinder TSA formula:** TSA = 2πr(r + h).
- **Step 3 — Substitute the known values:** TSA = 2 × (22/7) × 5 × (5 + 21).
- **Step 4 — Simplify the bracket:** 5 + 21 = 26.
- **Step 5 — Multiply step by step:** 2 × 22/7 = 44/7; 44/7 × 5 = 220/7; 220/7 × 26 = 5720/7.
- **Step 6 — Divide out:** 5720 ÷ 7 = 817.142857…
- **Answer: TSA ≈ 817.14 cm².**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- For a **closed** cylinder, always factor first — TSA = 2πr(r + h) is faster to compute than expanding 2πr² + 2πrh separately, because you only multiply once at the end instead of adding two products.
- Memorise the "half-and-double" trap: diameter given but the formula needs radius — halve it *before* you substitute, not after, to avoid restarting the calculation.
- For a cone, always check whether the question gives the **slant height** or the **vertical height** before you touch the TSA formula — TSA = πR(R + l) needs *l* (slant), never *h* (vertical); if only h is given, find l via l² = h² + R² first.
- Spot Pythagorean triples (3-4-5, 5-12-13, 7-24-25, 8-15-17, 9-12-15, 20-21-29) inside cone problems — if R and h match one, l is the third number instantly, with no calculator needed.
- Sanity-check any TSA answer: it must always be larger than the curved/lateral surface area alone (since TSA adds the flat end(s) on top of the curved part) — if your TSA comes out smaller than your CSA, you've made an arithmetic slip.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. What is the "net" of a solid? (answer: the 2-D shape obtained by unfolding all its faces flat)
Q2. Write the formula for the TSA of a cube of side l. (answer: TSA = 6l²)
Q3. A cube has side 5 cm. Find its TSA. (answer: 150 cm²)
Q4. Write the formula for the TSA of a cuboid. (answer: TSA = 2(lw + lh + wh))
Q5. A room (cuboid) is 10 m long, 8 m wide, 3 m high. Find the total area of the 4 walls and the ceiling (excluding the floor). (answer: walls = 2(10+8)(3) = 108 m², ceiling = 10×8 = 80 m², total = 188 m²)
Q6. Find the TSA of a cuboid of length 8 cm, width 5 cm, height 4 cm. (answer: 184 cm²)
Q7. Write the formula for the CSA of a cylinder. (answer: CSA = 2πrh)
Q8. A closed cylindrical tin has radius 7 cm and height 10 cm. Find its TSA (π = 22/7). (answer: 748 cm²)
Q9. A cylinder has radius 5 cm and CSA 100π cm². Find (a) its height, (b) its TSA. (answer: (a) h = 10 cm; (b) TSA = 150π cm²)
Q10. Find the TSA of a closed cylinder of diameter 10 cm and height 21 cm (π = 22/7). (answer: 817.14 cm²)
Q11. What is the total surface area of a closed cylinder of height 10 cm and diameter 7 cm (π = 22/7)? (answer: 297 cm²)
Q12. A cylinder with radius 3.5 cm has both ends closed. If its TSA is 209 cm², find its height (π = 22/7). (answer: 6 cm)
Q13. A solid cylindrical object of radius 7 cm is 10 cm high. Find its TSA (π = 22/7). (answer: 748 cm²)
Q14. A cylindrical tin with base diameter 14 cm and height 20 cm is open at the top. Find its TSA (π = 22/7). (answer: 1034 cm²)
Q15. The total surface area of a solid cylinder is 88 cm², with height 7 cm. Find, to 3 s.f., its diameter (π = 22/7). (answer: radius ≈ 1.625 cm, diameter ≈ 3.25 cm)
Q16. The curved surface area of a cylindrical tin is 704 cm² and its radius is 8 cm. Find its height (π = 22/7). (answer: 14 cm)
Q17. Write the formula for the TSA of a cone in terms of R and l. (answer: TSA = πR(R + l))
Q18. What formula connects l, h, and R in a cone? (answer: l² = h² + R²)
Q19. A cone has R = 3, h = 4. Find its slant height l. (answer: 5)
Q20. Find the TSA of a cone with base radius 5 cm and vertical height 12 cm (leave in terms of π). (answer: 90π cm² ≈ 282.7 cm²)
Q21. A cone has slant height 10 cm and base radius 6 cm. Find (a) its vertical height, (b) its TSA (in terms of π). (answer: (a) h = 8 cm; (b) TSA = 96π cm²)
Q22. A sector of a circle of radius 15 cm and angle 216° is folded into a cone. Find (a) the base radius, (b) the vertical height. (answer: (a) R = 9 cm; (b) h = 12 cm)
Q23. Write the formula for the TSA of a prism. (answer: TSA = 2(base area) + (base perimeter × length))
Q24. A square-based pyramid has base side 10 cm and slant height (of a triangular face) 12 cm. Find its TSA. (answer: 340 cm²)
Q25. What is the difference between the vertical height and the slant height of a cone or pyramid? (answer: vertical height goes straight up from the apex to the centre of the base; slant height runs along a triangular/curved face from the apex to the edge of the base)
Q26. A triangular prism has a cross-section that is a right triangle of legs 5 cm and 12 cm, and length 20 cm. Find its TSA. (answer: hypotenuse = √(5²+12²) = 13 cm, TSA = 2(½×5×12) + (5+12+13)(20) = 60 + 600 = 660 cm²)
Q27. A cylindrical drum has radius 50 cm and height 1.2 m. State its TSA formula and identify which quantities you need. (answer: TSA = 2πr(r+h); need r and h in the same units)
Q28. State the CSA formula for an open-top cylinder. (answer: CSA is the same for open or closed: 2πrh; only TSA differs)
Q29. A hemisphere of radius r sits on top of a cylinder of the same radius. Which surfaces are "hidden" and excluded from the total exposed surface area? (answer: the circular top of the cylinder and the flat circular base of the hemisphere, since they are joined together)
Q30. Two similar cylindrical jugs have radii in ratio 3:7. What is the ratio of their curved surface areas? (answer: CSA = 2πrh ∝ r for similar shapes, so the ratio is 3:7)

---

#### Week 2: Mensuration of 3-D Shapes II — Volume of Prisms, Pyramids, Cylinders, Cones, and Compound Solids

**Teaching Notes**

**Volume** is the amount of 3-D space a solid occupies (its "capacity"), measured in cubic units (cm³, m³). 1 litre = 1000 cm³.

**Prisms (uniform cross-section):** Volume = Base area × height (or length).
- Cube: V = l³
- Cuboid: V = lwh
- Cylinder: V = πr²h
- Triangular prism: V = (½bH) × L, where ½bH is the triangular cross-section area.

**Pyramids and cones (tapering solids):** the volume of any pyramid or cone is exactly one-third the volume of the prism/cylinder with the same base and height:

**V = ⅓ × Base area × h**
- Square-based pyramid: V = ⅓l²h
- Cone: V = ⅓πr²h (h must be the vertical height, never the slant height)

**Sphere and hemisphere** (radius r): V(sphere) = (4/3)πr³; V(hemisphere) = (2/3)πr³.

**Compound shapes** are made of two or more simple solids joined together. Total volume = sum of the volumes of each part.

**Worked example 1:** A cylindrical oil drum has radius 0.7 m and height 1.5 m (π = 22/7). Find its capacity in litres.
- **Step 1 — Write the volume formula for a cylinder:** V = πr²h.
- **Step 2 — Square the radius first:** r² = 0.7² = 0.49 m².
- **Step 3 — Substitute all values:** V = (22/7) × 0.49 × 1.5.
- **Step 4 — Multiply 22/7 by 0.49 (notice 0.49 = 0.07 × 7, which cancels neatly with the 7 on the bottom):** (22/7) × 0.49 = 22 × 0.07 = 1.54.
- **Step 5 — Multiply by the height:** 1.54 × 1.5 = 2.31.
- **Step 6 — State the volume in m³:** V = 2.31 m³.
- **Step 7 — Convert to litres, using 1 m³ = 1000 litres:** 2.31 × 1000 = 2310.
- **Answer: capacity = 2310 litres.**

**Worked example 2:** A cone has volume 110 cm³ and height 21 cm (π = 22/7). Find its base radius.
- **Step 1 — Write the cone volume formula:** V = ⅓πr²h.
- **Step 2 — Substitute the known values:** 110 = ⅓ × (22/7) × r² × 21.
- **Step 3 — Simplify the constants first (⅓ × 21 = 7):** 110 = (22/7) × 7 × r².
- **Step 4 — Cancel the 7s:** 110 = 22r².
- **Step 5 — Divide both sides by 22 to isolate r²:** r² = 110/22 = 5.
- **Step 6 — Take the square root of both sides:** r = √5.
- **Step 7 — Evaluate as a decimal:** √5 ≈ 2.236.
- **Answer: r = √5 cm ≈ 2.24 cm.**

**Worked example 3:** A square-based pyramid has base side 10 cm and slant height (of a triangular face) 13 cm. Find its volume.
- **Step 1 — Recall that pyramid volume needs the *vertical* height, not the slant height, so find it first.**
- **Step 2 — Find the apothem (the horizontal distance from the centre of the square base to the midpoint of a side):** apothem = base side ÷ 2 = 10/2 = 5 cm.
- **Step 3 — The slant height, the apothem, and the vertical height form a right triangle (the slant height is the hypotenuse):** h² = (slant height)² − (apothem)² = 13² − 5².
- **Step 4 — Compute the squares:** 13² = 169, 5² = 25.
- **Step 5 — Subtract:** h² = 169 − 25 = 144.
- **Step 6 — Take the square root:** h = √144 = 12 cm.
- **Step 7 — Find the base area:** base area = 10 × 10 = 100 cm².
- **Step 8 — Substitute into V = ⅓ × base area × h:** V = ⅓ × 100 × 12.
- **Step 9 — Simplify (12 ÷ 3 = 4 first, to avoid fractions):** V = 100 × 4 = 400.
- **Answer: V = 400 cm³.**

**Worked example 4:** A hemisphere of radius 7 cm sits on a cylinder of radius 7 cm, height 10 cm (π = 22/7). Find the total volume.
- **Step 1 — Recognise this is a compound solid — find each part's volume separately, then add.**
- **Step 2 — Find the cylinder's volume, V = πr²h:** V(cylinder) = (22/7) × 7² × 10 = (22/7) × 49 × 10.
- **Step 3 — Cancel the 7s (49 ÷ 7 = 7):** = 22 × 7 × 10 = 1540 cm³.
- **Step 4 — Find the hemisphere's volume, V = (2/3)πr³ (half of a full sphere's (4/3)πr³):** r³ = 7³ = 343.
- **Step 5 — Substitute:** V(hemisphere) = (2/3) × (22/7) × 343.
- **Step 6 — Cancel the 7 with 343 (343 ÷ 7 = 49):** = (2/3) × 22 × 49.
- **Step 7 — Multiply 22 × 49 = 1078, then multiply by 2:** 1078 × 2 = 2156.
- **Step 8 — Divide by 3:** 2156 ÷ 3 = 718.67 cm³ (2 d.p.).
- **Step 9 — Add the two volumes together:** Total = 1540 + 718.67 = 2258.67 cm³.
- **Answer: Total volume ≈ 2258.67 cm³.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- Always cancel π's denominator (7, when using 22/7) against a matching factor in r or r² *before* multiplying everything out — it avoids messy decimals and is much faster by hand.
- 1 m³ = 1000 litres and 1 cm³ = 1 mL, so once you have a volume in m³ or cm³, converting to litres is just a shift of the decimal point (÷1000 for cm³→litres is NOT needed — only m³→litres ×1000, or cm³→litres ÷1000). Keep the two straight: cm³ ÷ 1000 = litres; m³ × 1000 = litres.
- For "find the radius/height given the volume" problems, isolate the unknown squared or cubed term first, simplify the constants, and only take the square/cube root as your very last step — never round early.
- Pyramid and cone volumes are always exactly ⅓ of the "matching" prism/cylinder with the same base and height — a fast mental check: if a cone and cylinder share R and h, the cone's volume should come out to almost exactly one-third of the cylinder's.
- For compound solids (hemisphere-on-cylinder, cone-on-cylinder, etc.), always compute and label each part's volume *separately* before adding — mixing formulas into one giant expression is where most marks are lost to arithmetic slips.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. State the general formula for the volume of any prism. (answer: V = Base area × height)
Q2. State the general formula for the volume of any pyramid or cone. (answer: V = ⅓ × Base area × height)
Q3. Write the formula for the volume of a cuboid. (answer: V = lwh)
Q4. A cube has side 4 cm. Find its volume. (answer: 64 cm³)
Q5. Write the formula for the volume of a cylinder. (answer: V = πr²h)
Q6. A cylindrical oil drum has radius 0.7 m and height 1.5 m (π = 22/7). How many litres does it hold? (answer: 2310 litres)
Q7. A cylindrical tank of length 24 cm has volume 14 784 cm³. Find its radius (π = 22/7). (answer: 14 cm)
Q8. A cylindrical container of base radius 4 cm has volume 352 cm³. Find its depth (π = 22/7). (answer: 7 cm)
Q9. The height of a cylinder equals its radius; its volume is 0.216 m³. Find the radius. (answer: 0.6 m)
Q10. A cylindrical tank with diameter 1 m and height 2 m is half filled with water. Find the volume of water (in terms of π). (answer: π/4 m³)
Q11. A tin of milk has height 10.3 cm and base radius 6.8 cm (π = 22/7). Find the volume of liquid it holds, in litres. (answer: 1.497 litres)
Q12. Find the capacity in litres of a cylinder of diameter 18 cm and height 17 cm (π = 22/7). (answer: 4327.71 cm³ ≈ 4.328 litres)
Q13. The volume of a cylinder is 1200 cm³ and its base area is 150 cm². Find its height. (answer: 8 cm)
Q14. The radii of two similar cylindrical jugs are in ratio 3:7. Find the ratio of their volumes. (answer: 27:343)
Q15. Two similar cylinders have volumes in ratio 216:125; the taller one is 30 cm high. Find the height of the smaller one. (answer: 15 cm)
Q16. Write the formula for the volume of a cone. (answer: V = ⅓πr²h)
Q17. To find the volume of a cone, do you need the slant height or the vertical height? (answer: the vertical height)
Q18. A cone has volume 110 cm³ and height 21 cm (π = 22/7). Find its base radius. (answer: √5 ≈ 2.24 cm)
Q19. Find the volume of a cone with radius 7 cm and slant height 25 cm (π = 22/7). (answer: h = √(25²−7²) = √(625−49) = √576 = 24 cm; V = ⅓×(22/7)×49×24 = 1232 cm³)
Q20. A cylindrical container 20 cm deep holds 3.08 litres of water. Find its radius (π = 3080/3.14… use π = 22/7; V = 3080 cm³). (answer: 7 cm)
Q21. A cone and a cylinder have the same radius and height. If the cylinder's volume is 30 m³, what is the cone's volume? (answer: 10 m³)
Q22. State the relationship between the volume of a cone and a cylinder with the same base and height. (answer: cone = ⅓ × cylinder)
Q23. A square-based pyramid has base side 10 cm and slant height 13 cm. Find its volume. (answer: 400 cm³)
Q24. A square-based pyramid has base side 18 cm and vertical height 12 cm. Find its volume. (answer: 1296 cm³)
Q25. A triangular prism has a right-triangular base of legs 5 cm and 12 cm, and length 20 cm. Find its volume. (answer: 600 cm³)
Q26. A hemisphere of radius 7 cm sits atop a cylinder of radius 7 cm, height 10 cm (π = 22/7). Find the total volume. (answer: 2258.67 cm³)
Q27. A pyramid stands on a cuboid of dimensions 4 m × 3 m × 2 m; the slant edge of the pyramid is 5 m. Find the total volume of the shape. (answer: 41.32 m³)
Q28. How many cm³ are in 1 litre? (answer: 1000 cm³)
Q29. A drum of radius 50 cm and height 1.2 m is filled with water using a bucket of top radius 15 cm, bottom radius 10 cm, height 50 cm (π = 22/7). How many bucket-fuls fill the drum, to the nearest whole bucket? (answer: 38 buckets)
Q30. A bucket holds 10 litres of water. How many buckets fill a reservoir of size 8 m × 7 m × 5 m? (answer: 28 000 buckets)
Q31. A rectangular tank 11 m × 2 m × 7 m has the same volume as a cylindrical tank of height 4 m. Find the cylinder's radius (π = 22/7). (answer: 3.5 m)

#### Week 3: Volume and Surface Area of Frustums (Cones and Pyramids)

**Teaching Notes**

A **frustum** is the solid left when the top of a cone or pyramid is sliced off by a cut parallel to the base (e.g. a bucket, a lampshade, a flower vase). The standard strategy is: **"Large solid minus Small solid."**

**Finding the missing height.** Let R = radius (or half-base) of the large base, r = radius of the small (top) face, H = height of the complete original cone/pyramid, h_s = height of the removed small cone/pyramid, h_f = height of the frustum, so H = h_s + h_f. Because the small cone/pyramid is similar to the large one:

**r/R = h_s/H**

Solve this (usually via cross-multiplication) to find h_s, then H = h_s + h_f.

**Volume of a frustum (cone):**

**V(frustum) = V(large cone) − V(small cone) = ⅓πR²H − ⅓πr²h_s**

This is also equal to the direct formula: **V(frustum) = (π/3)h_f(R² + Rr + r²)**

**Volume of a frustum (pyramid):** V(frustum) = ⅓(Base area)H − ⅓(top area)h_s, using the large and small base areas.

**Surface area of a frustum (cone):** CSA = πRL − πrl_s (difference of the two cones' curved surfaces, where L and l_s are the large and small slant heights); TSA = πR² + πr² + CSA (add the two flat circular ends).

**Worked example 1:** A bucket is 28 cm high (frustum height); top and bottom radii are 10 cm and 15 cm (π = 22/7). Find its volume.
- **Step 1 — Sketch the idea:** the bucket is a large cone (radius 15 cm) with a small cone (radius 10 cm) sliced off the top. Let h_s = height of the small cone that was removed, H = height of the whole original cone.
- **Step 2 — Set up the similar-triangle ratio (small radius/large radius = small height/large height):** r/R = h_s/H, i.e. 10/15 = h_s/(h_s + 28) (since H = h_s + 28).
- **Step 3 — Cross-multiply:** 15 × h_s = 10 × (h_s + 28).
- **Step 4 — Expand the right side:** 15h_s = 10h_s + 280.
- **Step 5 — Collect h_s terms on one side:** 15h_s − 10h_s = 280, so 5h_s = 280.
- **Step 6 — Solve for h_s:** h_s = 280/5 = 56 cm.
- **Step 7 — Find the full height H:** H = h_s + 28 = 56 + 28 = 84 cm.
- **Step 8 — Find the volume of the large (complete) cone, V = ⅓πR²H:** V(large) = ⅓ × (22/7) × 15² × 84 = ⅓ × (22/7) × 225 × 84.
- **Step 9 — Simplify (84 ÷ 7 = 12, then ÷3 = 4):** = 22 × 225 × 4 = 19 800 cm³.
- **Step 10 — Find the volume of the small (removed) cone, V = ⅓πr²h_s:** V(small) = ⅓ × (22/7) × 10² × 56 = ⅓ × (22/7) × 100 × 56.
- **Step 11 — Simplify (56 ÷ 7 = 8):** = ⅓ × 22 × 100 × 8 = ⅓ × 17 600 = 5866.67 cm³.
- **Step 12 — Subtract small from large:** V(frustum) = 19 800 − 5866.67 = 13 933.33 cm³.
- **Answer: V(frustum) ≈ 13 933.33 cm³.**

**Worked example 2:** A frustum of a cone has top radius 6 cm, bottom radius 8 cm, and frustum height 10 cm. Find the height of the whole cone and the frustum's volume (π = 22/7).
- **Step 1 — Let x = height of the small cone cut off; set up the similar-triangle ratio:** 6/8 = x/(x + 10).
- **Step 2 — Cross-multiply:** 8x = 6(x + 10).
- **Step 3 — Expand:** 8x = 6x + 60.
- **Step 4 — Collect x terms:** 8x − 6x = 60, so 2x = 60.
- **Step 5 — Solve:** x = 30 cm.
- **Step 6 — Find the total height:** H = x + 10 = 30 + 10 = 40 cm.
- **Step 7 — Use the direct frustum-volume shortcut formula (avoids computing two full cones):** V = (π/3) × h_f × (R² + Rr + r²), with h_f = 10, R = 8, r = 6.
- **Step 8 — Compute R², Rr, r²:** R² = 64, Rr = 48, r² = 36.
- **Step 9 — Add them:** 64 + 48 + 36 = 148.
- **Step 10 — Substitute:** V = (22/21) × 10 × 148.
- **Step 11 — Multiply 10 × 148 = 1480, then × 22 = 32 560.**
- **Step 12 — Divide by 21:** 32 560 ÷ 21 ≈ 1550.48.
- **Answer: H = 40 cm; V(frustum) ≈ 1550.48 cm³.**

**Worked example 3:** A frustum of a square-based pyramid has top side 4 m, bottom side 12 m, height 10 m. Find (i) the height of the whole pyramid, (ii) the frustum's volume.
- **Step 1 — Set up the similar-triangle ratio using the *sides* (since the base is square, side plays the role radius does for a cone):** 4/12 = x/(x + 10), where x is the height of the small pyramid removed.
- **Step 2 — Simplify the fraction 4/12 = 1/3 first, to make the algebra easier:** 1/3 = x/(x + 10).
- **Step 3 — Cross-multiply:** x + 10 = 3x.
- **Step 4 — Collect x terms:** 10 = 3x − x = 2x.
- **Step 5 — Solve:** x = 5 m.
- **Step 6 — Find the full pyramid height:** H = x + 10 = 5 + 10 = 15 m.
- **Step 7 — Find the volume of the large pyramid, V = ⅓ × base area × H:** base area = 12² = 144 m²; V(large) = ⅓ × 144 × 15.
- **Step 8 — Simplify (15 ÷ 3 = 5):** = 144 × 5 = 720 m³.
- **Step 9 — Find the volume of the small pyramid removed:** top area = 4² = 16 m²; V(small) = ⅓ × 16 × 5.
- **Step 10 — Simplify:** = 16 × 5/3 = 80/3 ≈ 26.67 m³.
- **Step 11 — Subtract:** V(frustum) = 720 − 26.67 = 693.33 m³.
- **Answer: (i) H = 15 m; (ii) V(frustum) ≈ 693.33 m³.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- The single most important first move on any frustum problem is drawing the "phantom" small cone/pyramid above the frustum — labelling H (whole), h_s (removed top), and h_f (frustum) prevents sign errors in the similar-triangle equation.
- When cross-multiplying r/R = h_s/H with H = h_s + h_f, simplify the r/R fraction to lowest terms first (e.g. 4/12 → 1/3) — smaller numbers mean faster, safer algebra.
- For volume, use the direct formula V = (π/3)h(R² + Rr + r²) instead of "large cone minus small cone" whenever you already know h_f, R, and r directly — it's one substitution instead of two full volume calculations and a subtraction.
- Sanity-check every frustum answer: it must always be **less** than the volume of the large cone/pyramid alone (computed with the *total* height H) and **more** than zero — if your frustum volume is negative or bigger than the large solid, a height or ratio was set up upside-down.
- For TSA of a frustum, remember it needs *four* pieces added together: the two circular (or square) end areas *plus* the curved/lateral surface — a common exam error is forgetting one of the two flat ends.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. What is a frustum? (answer: the part of a cone or pyramid left after slicing off the top parallel to the base)
Q2. What key mathematical principle is used to find the "missing" height of the original cone/pyramid? (answer: similar triangles)
Q3. Write the similar-triangle ratio linking the radii and heights of a cone frustum. (answer: r/R = h_s/H)
Q4. Write the direct volume formula for a cone frustum. (answer: V = (π/3)h(R² + Rr + r²))
Q5. Write the formula for the TSA of a cone frustum. (answer: TSA = πR² + πr² + π(RL − rl_s))
Q6. A frustum of a cone has top radius 5 cm, bottom radius 10 cm, vertical height 12 cm. Find (a) the height of the small cone cut off, (b) the volume of the frustum. (answer: (a) h_s = 12 cm (since 5/10 = 12/H gives H=24, so h_s = H−12 = 12 cm); (b) V(large)=⅓π(10²)(24)=800π, V(small)=⅓π(5²)(12)=100π, V(frustum)=800π−100π=700π ≈ 2199.1 cm³)
Q7. A frustum is cut from a square-based pyramid; large base 15 cm, small base 5 cm, frustum height 6 cm. Find (a) the height of the original pyramid, (b) the volume of the frustum. (answer: (a) 5/15 = h_s/(h_s+6) gives h_s = 3 cm, so H = 9 cm; (b) V(large)=⅓(15²)(9)=675 cm³, V(small)=⅓(5²)(3)=25 cm³, V(frustum)=675−25 = 650 cm³)
Q8. A bucket is 28 cm high with top and bottom radii 15 cm and 10 cm (π = 22/7). Find its volume. (answer: 13 933.33 cm³, or 41 800/3 cm³ exactly)
Q9. A part of a cone has top radius 4 cm, bottom radius 8 cm, slant height 5 cm. Find its vertical height. (answer: 3 cm)
Q10. A frustum of a pyramid with square base has top and bottom squares of side 2 m and 5 m, and the sections are 6 m apart. Find the height of the whole pyramid. (answer: 10 m)
Q11. A frustum of a pyramid with square base has top and bottom squares of side 7 m and 13 m, 10 m apart. Find the height of the whole pyramid. (answer: 7/13 = h_s/(h_s+10) gives 13h_s = 7h_s+70, 6h_s=70, h_s ≈ 11.67 m, so H = h_s+10 ≈ 21.67 m)
Q12. Find the height of the cone cut off to leave a frustum with top radius 4 cm and bottom radius 8 cm, height of frustum 6 cm. (answer: 6 cm)
Q13. A frustum has top radius 6 cm, bottom radius 8 cm, frustum height 10 cm. Find (i) the height of the whole cone, (ii) the volume of the frustum, to the nearest whole number (π = 22/7). (answer: (i) 40 cm; (ii) 1550 cm³)
Q14. A bucket is 28 cm in diameter at the top, 18 cm in diameter at the bottom, and 20 cm deep. Find its capacity in litres (π = 3.142). (answer: R=14, r=9, h=20; V=(π/3)h(R²+Rr+r²)=(3.142/3)(20)(196+126+81)=(3.142/3)(20)(403)≈8441.65 cm³ ≈ 8.44 litres)
Q15. A flower vase 8 cm high is a frustum of a square-based pyramid, side 6 cm at the bottom and 10 cm at the top. Find the volume of water it holds when full. (answer: using V=(h/3)(A1+A2+√(A1A2)) with A1=6²=36, A2=10²=100: V=(8/3)(36+100+60)=(8/3)(196)≈522.67 cm³)
Q16. A frustum of a square-based pyramid is 4 m at the top and 12 m at the bottom, height 10 m. Find (i) the height of the whole pyramid, (ii) the volume of the frustum to the nearest whole number. (answer: (i) 15 m; (ii) 693 m³)
Q17. A drum of radius 50 cm and height 1.2 m is filled using a frustum-shaped bucket (top radius 15 cm, bottom radius 10 cm, height 50 cm). Find the number of buckets needed to fill the drum, to the nearest whole bucket (π = 22/7). (answer: 38 buckets)
Q18. When finding the total surface area of a compound shape (e.g. a cone sitting on a cylinder), what must you be careful not to include? (answer: the "hidden" joined surfaces — e.g. the cone's base and the cylinder's top, which are not exposed)
Q19. A solid object is made of a cylinder of height 10 cm and radius 3 cm, topped by a hemisphere of radius 3 cm. Find (a) the total volume, (b) the total surface area. (answer: (a) V = π(3²)(10) + (2/3)π(3³) = 90π + 18π = 108π ≈ 339.3 cm³; (b) TSA = CSA(cylinder) + base circle + CSA(hemisphere) = 2π(3)(10) + π(3²) + 2π(3²) = 60π + 9π + 18π = 87π ≈ 273.3 cm²)
Q20. State the "large minus small" volume strategy for any frustum problem in one sentence. (answer: volume of frustum = volume of the complete large solid − volume of the small solid that was removed from the top)

#### Week 4: Angle Sum of a Triangle (= 180°) and the Exterior Angle Theorem

**Teaching Notes**

An **axiom** (postulate) is a statement accepted as true without proof; a **theorem** is a statement proved true using logic and axioms. A formal proof has the structure: **Given** (what's known) → **To Prove** (the goal) → **Construction** (an added helper line, if needed) → **Proof** (a step-by-step argument, with a reason for every statement) → **Conclusion** (Q.E.D.).

**Theorem 1: The sum of the angles in a triangle is 180°.**
Given triangle ABC. Construction: draw a line XY through A, parallel to BC.
Proof: ∠XAB = ∠ABC (alternate angles, XY∥BC); ∠YAC = ∠ACB (alternate angles); ∠XAB + ∠BAC + ∠YAC = 180° (angles on a straight line XY). Substituting: ∠ABC + ∠BAC + ∠ACB = 180°. Q.E.D.

**Theorem 2: The exterior angle of a triangle equals the sum of the two opposite (remote) interior angles.**
Given triangle ABC with BC extended to D. To prove: exterior angle ACD = ∠A + ∠B.
Proof: ∠A + ∠B + ∠ACB = 180° (Theorem 1); ∠ACB + ∠ACD = 180° (angles on line BCD). Both equal 180°, so ∠A + ∠B + ∠ACB = ∠ACB + ∠ACD; subtracting ∠ACB from both sides gives ∠A + ∠B = ∠ACD. Q.E.D.

**Worked example 1:** In a triangle, angles are 2x, 3x, and 40°. Find x.
- **Step 1 — Apply the angle-sum theorem:** the three angles of any triangle add to 180°, so 2x + 3x + 40 = 180.
- **Step 2 — Collect the like (x) terms on the left:** 2x + 3x = 5x, so the equation becomes 5x + 40 = 180.
- **Step 3 — Subtract 40 from both sides to isolate the x-term:** 5x = 180 − 40 = 140.
- **Step 4 — Divide both sides by 5:** x = 140/5 = 28.
- **Step 5 — Check by substituting back:** angles are 2(28)=56°, 3(28)=84°, and 40°; sum = 56+84+40 = 180° ✓.
- **Answer: x = 28°.**

**Worked example 2:** In triangle ABC, ∠A = 50°, ∠B = 70°; find the exterior angle at C.
- **Step 1 — Identify which theorem applies:** the exterior angle at any vertex equals the sum of the two *remote* (non-adjacent) interior angles — here, the remote angles for the exterior angle at C are ∠A and ∠B.
- **Step 2 — Apply the theorem directly:** exterior angle at C = ∠A + ∠B = 50° + 70°.
- **Step 3 — Add:** 50 + 70 = 120.
- **Step 4 — Cross-check using the straight-line method:** first find ∠C using the angle-sum rule: ∠C = 180 − 50 − 70 = 60°; the exterior angle at C is then 180 − 60 = 120° (angles on a straight line) — this matches Step 3, confirming the answer.
- **Answer: exterior angle at C = 120°.**

**Worked example 3:** In triangle PQR, ∠P = 2x, ∠Q = 3x, and the exterior angle at R is 140°. Find x and all interior angles.
- **Step 1 — Identify the relevant theorem:** the exterior angle at R equals the sum of the two remote interior angles, ∠P and ∠Q.
- **Step 2 — Write the equation:** 140 = 2x + 3x.
- **Step 3 — Simplify the right side:** 2x + 3x = 5x, so 140 = 5x.
- **Step 4 — Solve for x:** x = 140/5 = 28°.
- **Step 5 — Find ∠P:** ∠P = 2x = 2 × 28 = 56°.
- **Step 6 — Find ∠Q:** ∠Q = 3x = 3 × 28 = 84°.
- **Step 7 — Find ∠R using the fact that the exterior angle and interior angle at the same vertex are supplementary (sum to 180°, since they lie on a straight line):** ∠R = 180 − 140 = 40°.
- **Step 8 — Verify with the angle-sum theorem:** 56° + 84° + 40° = 180° ✓.
- **Answer: x = 28°; ∠P = 56°, ∠Q = 84°, ∠R = 40°.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- If a question gives you the exterior angle and just one remote interior angle, subtract directly — no need to find the third angle first: missing remote angle = exterior angle − known remote angle.
- Any time you know two angles of a triangle in an exam, the third is always just 180° minus their sum — you never need extra construction lines for this, only for formal *proofs*.
- To find the exterior angle at any vertex, you can use *either* "sum of the two remote interior angles" or "180° minus the interior angle at that vertex" — doing both is a fast, free way to double-check your working in an exam.
- Watch for triangles described by ratios (e.g. 2:3:4) — add the ratio parts (2+3+4=9), divide 180° by that total to get the value of "one part" (20°), then multiply each ratio number by that value; this is much faster than writing simultaneous equations.
- If you're ever unsure whether a set of three given angles could form a real triangle, just add them: only sets that sum to exactly 180° are valid.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. What is the difference between an axiom and a theorem? (answer: an axiom is accepted as true without proof; a theorem is proved true using logic from axioms)
Q2. What does "Q.E.D." stand for? (answer: quod erat demonstrandum — "what was to be shown")
Q3. In the proof that angles in a triangle sum to 180°, what construction line is drawn? (answer: a line through one vertex, parallel to the opposite side)
Q4. Which angle rule connects the triangle's angles to the straight-line angles in that proof? (answer: alternate angles between parallel lines, and angles on a straight line summing to 180°)
Q5. State the theorem about the exterior angle of a triangle. (answer: it equals the sum of the two interior opposite angles)
Q6. What two rules combine to prove the exterior angle theorem? (answer: angle sum of a triangle = 180°, and angles on a straight line = 180°)
Q7. Find x if a triangle's angles are 2x, 3x, 40°. (answer: x = 28°, so angles are 56°, 84°, 40°)
Q8. Find y: a triangle has angles 50° and 70°, and an exterior angle y at the third vertex. (answer: y = 120°)
Q9. One angle in a right triangle is 90°; the others are x and 2x. Find x. (answer: x = 30°)
Q10. The exterior angle of a triangle is 110°; one opposite interior angle is 40°. Find the other. (answer: 70°)
Q11. Can a triangle have angles 50°, 60°, and 80°? Why or why not? (answer: no — they sum to 190°, not 180°)
Q12. The angles of a triangle are in the ratio 2:3:4. Find each angle. (answer: 40°, 60°, 80°)
Q13. In triangle PQR, ∠P = 2x, ∠Q = 3x, and the exterior angle at R is 140°. Find x and all three interior angles. (answer: x = 28°; ∠P=56°, ∠Q=84°, ∠R=40°)
Q14. Write out, with reasons, the full proof that the angle sum of a triangle is 180°. (answer: see Teaching Notes — Given/To Prove/Construction/Proof/Q.E.D.)
Q15. A triangle has one angle of 90°. If the other two are in the ratio 1:2, find them. (answer: 30° and 60°)
Q16. Find x: the angles of a triangle are (2x+10)°, (3x−5)°, and (x+25)°. (answer: x = 30°, giving angles 70°, 85°, and 25°)
Q17. In a triangle, the exterior angle at one vertex is 3 times one remote interior angle (35°) and the other remote interior angle is y. If the exterior angle is 105°, find y. (answer: exterior angle = sum of remote interior angles, so 105 = 35 + y, giving y = 70°)
Q18. A triangle's interior angles are x, x+10°, and x+20°. Find x and the largest angle. (answer: x+(x+10)+(x+20)=180, so 3x+30=180, x=50°; largest angle = x+20 = 70°)
Q19. State, in your own words, why an exterior angle of a triangle is always greater than either remote interior angle. (answer: because it equals their sum, and both angles are positive, so it must exceed each one individually)
Q20. Two angles of a triangle are equal (isosceles) and the third is 40° larger than each. Find all three angles. (answer: 2x + (x+40) = 180, 3x = 140, x ≈ 46.7°; angles ≈ 46.7°, 46.7°, 86.7°)
